It provides compelling evidence demonstrating reliable\\nlongitudinal identification of neurons across the second postnatal week in mice. The\\nstudy should be of interest to development neuroscientists engaged in population-\\nlevel recordings using two-photon imaging.\\nhttps://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2.sa4\\nAbstract\\nUnderstanding cortical circuit development requires tracking neuronal activity across days in\\nthe growing brain. While in vivo calcium imaging now enables such longitudinal studies,\\nautomated tools for reliably tracking large populations of neurons across sessions remain\\nlimited. Here, we present a novel cell-tracking method based on sequential image\\nregistration, validated on calcium imaging data from the barrel cortex of mouse pups over\\none postnatal week. Our approach enables robust long-term analysis of several hundreds of\\nindividual neurons, allowing quantification of neuronal dynamics and representational\\nstability over time. Using this method, we identified a key developmental transition in\\nneuronal activity statistics, marking the emergence of arousal state modulation. Beyond this\\nkey finding, our method provides an essential tool for tracking developmental trajectories of\\nindividual neurons, which could help identify potential deviations associated with\\nneurodevelopmental disorders.\\nIntroduction\\nEarly postnatal development in rodents is a period of intense circuit wiring and remodelling at\\nseveral scales through various major processes that include neuronal growth, synaptogenesis,\\napoptosis, migration, rise of intracortical connectivity, functional maturation of inhibitory\\nReviewed Preprint\\nv2 • October 27, 2025\\nRevised by authors\\nReviewed Preprint\\nv1 • July 9, 2025\\n\\n--- PAGE 2 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n2 of 46\\nsynapses and the disappearance of transient connectivity schemes 1     –4     . Critical periods for\\nvarious sensory systems open and close during this time, further highlighting the profound\\nreshaping of cortical networks 4     . This developmental period of remodelling is crucial for\\nestablishing the functional architecture of the mature cortex. Most importantly, all of these\\ndevelopmental processes are activity-dependent, with collective dynamics playing a critical role in\\nthe proper integration of neurons into functional networks 5     . These network dynamics\\nsequentially unfold while relying on different mechanisms and circuits for their generation\\n1     ,5     . The emergence of functional brain circuits during development is therefore a precisely\\ntimed choreography, the timing of which is inherently tied to the age of the organism under study.\\nHowever, developmental age lacks precision due to significant variations in physical\\ncharacteristics and growth patterns, even among offspring from the same genetic lineage (Fig.\\n1     ). Hence, longitudinal imaging from the same animal across days is the optimal solution to\\nbetter capture the evolution of circuit dynamics during development. Additionally, developmental\\nvariability extends beyond the organism level, becoming even more pronounced at the single\\nneuron level (Fig. 1     ). While population-level descriptions of cortical circuit development are\\ncrucial, individual neurons exhibit unique developmental trajectories rooted in their specific\\norigin, birth timing and cellular identity 1     . In addition, the singular dynamics of sparse\\nindividual neurons can matter, as demonstrated for the rare hub cells 6     ,7     . Thus, in order to\\nfully understand the circuit basis of cortical development in health and disease, it is crucial to\\ntrack neuronal activity at both population and individual cell levels in the growing brain of the\\nsame animal. This hurdle spans both experimental methodology and data analysis.\\nSignificant progress has been made over the recent years to develop innovative solutions to meet\\nthis challenge on the experimental side, using two-photon calcium imaging. Technical\\nimprovements included modified head plates and specialized surgical and care protocols 6     ,8     –\\n12     . Still, in many cases, different neurons from the same mouse were recorded at different ages,\\nor the tracking of individual cells could only be achieved through visual inspection and manual\\nannotation of relatively sparse, often genetically-subscribed neuronal populations expressing a\\ncalcium fluorescent reporter. The gold standard for developmental circuit analysis would be\\nautomated tracking of densely labeled neuronal populations, enabling efficient longitudinal\\nmonitoring while eliminating the burden of manual cell tracking. An algorithm capable of\\naccurate automatic tracking of the same neurons across multiple days of\\n--- PAGE 3 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n3 of 46\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n3 of 46\\nFigure 1\\nTracking developmental trajectories at the organismal and cellular level.\\nPostnatal development in mammals is not a strictly stereotyped process (left) but rather shows variability across individual\\norganisms as well as across individual cells of the same organism (right). Performing an acute experiment (dashed grey line)\\nonly provides a single snapshot of the developmental trajectory of an individual organism (or cell). Alternatively, a\\nlongitudinal experiment (grey arrow) allows to track the properties of the same individual (or cell) throughout development,\\nwhich is especially important in the case of variability in developmental trajectories (right).\\n\\n--- PAGE 4 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n4 of 46\\nApplying the algorithm to a dataset recorded during the second postnatal week in mouse barrel\\ncortex, a critical period for the formation of topographic maps in that region, yielded hundreds of\\nidentified neurons tracked across all days. Assessing the quality of the algorithm by benchmarking\\nit on a newly generated ground truth dataset showed high tracking performance 20     . Leveraging\\nground truth benchmarking, we demonstrate that explicitly accounting for developmental\\nprocesses, such as brain growth, are critical for accurately tracking cells during postnatal\\ndevelopment. Our work thus shows that chronic calcium imaging and cell tracking using Track2p\\ncan be used to monitor the changing physiological properties of large populations of matched\\nneurons during early cortical development. We demonstrate that the statistics of activity patterns\\nin the tracked population display two periods of stability, with a critical transition point around\\npostnatal day 11 (P11), marking the emergence of a stable behavioral state representation.\\nResults\\nCell tracking using image registration and overlap-based matching\\nTracking neuronal activity across multiple days presents unique challenges due to the dynamic\\nnature of brain development. We developed a novel tracking algorithm, called Track2p. As in\\nother tracking algorithms for calcium imaging data 13     ,16     ,21     , the final goal of Track2p is to\\nfollow individual cells across sessions allowing the user to compare their functional properties in\\ndownstream analyses. To achieve this goal, the algorithm takes as input a set of preprocessed\\nrecordings, each consisting of a set of regions of interest (ROIs i.e. putative neurons detected based\\non activity, see Methods) and their respective calcium fluorescence traces, as well as a mean image\\nof the field of view (FOV). Briefly, the algorithm aims to match ROIs in any given pair of\\nconsecutive sessions based on their spatial overlap, assuming that the more the two overlap in\\nanatomical space, the more likely they correspond to the same neuron. Due to developmental\\nprocesses such as brain growth and other experimental factors, it is necessary to account for day-\\nto-day changes that occur between the two recordings before computing spatial overlaps. This is\\nachieved by performing affine image registration on the mean FOV images between consecutive\\ndays.\\nWe apply the registration and spatial matching iteratively, starting with the first pair of sessions\\n(s0 and s1) as follows (Fig. 2A     ):\\nFirstly, we estimate the spatial transformation between s0 and s1 using affine image registration\\n(Ntatsis 2023) (i.e. allowing shifting, rotation, scaling and shearing, see Fig. 2B     , the\\ntransformation is denoted as T). We employ affine transformation, since it can account both for\\nrigid transformations (rotations and translations arising from minor mismatches in FOV\\nalignment across experi\\n--- PAGE 5 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n5 of 46\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n5 of 46\\nFigure 2\\nThe Track2p pipeline for tracking cells across recordings.\\nA: Schematic overview of the Track2p algorithm and the GUI capabilities. Dashed squares represent optional steps in the\\npipeline. B: The transformation (T) between two consecutive days is computed by registering the mean FOV images. Top: An\\noverlay of the reference (red, s0) and the moving (green, s1) images before registration. Bottom: Same two images after\\nregistration. Scale bar: 50 μm. C: Applying the transform to ROIs from Suite2p segmentation. The ROI color code is the same\\nas in B, the intersection of the ROIs is shown in yellow. Top: Overlap before T. Bottom: Overlap after T. Note: Only a few\\nexample ROIs are displayed for explanation purposes. D: Cell matching using linear sum assignment. ROIs are the same as C\\nwith indexes for the two recordings added for illustrative purposes. Cells from one recording are matched to cells from\\nanother maximising the sum of the intersection over union (IoU) across all matches. This ensures that assigned matches with low spatial\\nsimilarity are rejected (see + in Fig. 2D and E     ) while the matches with high similarity are\\naccepted (see x in Fig. 2D and E     ). This yields the final matching for the first pair of consecutive\\nimaging sessions (s0 and s1). In the case of more than 2 recordings, this tracking procedure is then\\niteratively applied for all consecutive pairs of sessions (s0 to s1, s1 to s2 … sN-1 to sN, and so on),\\nwith tracks being extended sequentially from s0 to sN based on the identified matches, and\\nterminated if a match was not identified at any particular session (Fig. 2F     ).\\nWe provide an open-source implementation of the algorithm, combined with a user-friendly\\ngraphical user interface (GUI), allowing non-specialist users to run the algorithm and interact with\\nits outputs (see Fig. 2A      top, Fig. S1      and Supp. Info.: Software). Both the algorithm and the GUI\\ncome with a simple installation procedure and extensive documentation to facilitate the ease of\\nuse and accessibility of Track2p.\\nTracking neurons across days in the early postnatal neocortex\\nIn order to validate our method on experimental data, we next applied Track2p to a longitudinal\\ndataset consisting of daily recordings of the same 720×720 μm FOV throughout the second\\npostnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development (postnatal days 8 (P8) to P14, n=7 imaging\\nsessions from one mouse; for more details see Methods.) In addition to the calcium indicator\\n(GCaMP8m), we virally expressed a sparse anatomical marker (tdTomato) targeting GABAergic\\nneurons using conditional expression in GAD67-Cre mice 22     . This dual-labelling strategy\\nfacilitated reliable FOV identification across sessions and provided an anatomical reference for\\nTrack2p’s initial cross-session registration and tracking (Fig. 3     , for comparison with tracking\\nbased on GCaMP8m registration see the ‘Benchmarking on manually tracked cells’ section). Before\\nrunning Track2p, each recording was preprocessed using Suite2p 23      for active cell detection\\n(segmentation) and calcium fluorescence trace extraction (see Methods). It provides compelling evidence demonstrating reliable\\nlongitudinal identification of neurons across the second postnatal week in mice. The\\nstudy should be of interest to development neuroscientists engaged in population-\\nlevel recordings using two-photon imaging.\\nhttps://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2.sa4\\nAbstract\\nUnderstanding cortical circuit development requires tracking neuronal activity across days in\\nthe growing brain. While in vivo calcium imaging now enables such longitudinal studies,\\nautomated tools for reliably tracking large populations of neurons across sessions remain\\nlimited. Here, we present a novel cell-tracking method based on sequential image\\nregistration, validated on calcium imaging data from the barrel cortex of mouse pups over\\none postnatal week. Our approach enables robust long-term analysis of several hundreds of\\nindividual neurons, allowing quantification of neuronal dynamics and representational\\nstability over time. Using this method, we identified a key developmental transition in\\nneuronal activity statistics, marking the emergence of arousal state modulation. Beyond this\\nkey finding, our method provides an essential tool for tracking developmental trajectories of\\nindividual neurons, which could help identify potential deviations associated with\\nneurodevelopmental disorders.\\nIntroduction\\nEarly postnatal development in rodents is a period of intense circuit wiring and remodelling at\\nseveral scales through various major processes that include neuronal growth, synaptogenesis,\\napoptosis, migration, rise of intracortical connectivity, functional maturation of inhibitory\\nReviewed Preprint\\nv2 • October 27, 2025\\nRevised by authors\\nReviewed Preprint\\nv1 • July 9, 2025\\n\\n--- PAGE 2 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n2 of 46\\nsynapses and the disappearance of transient connectivity schemes 1     –4     . Critical periods for\\nvarious sensory systems open and close during this time, further highlighting the profound\\nreshaping of cortical networks 4     . This developmental period of remodelling is crucial for\\nestablishing the functional architecture of the mature cortex. Most importantly, all of these\\ndevelopmental processes are activity-dependent, with collective dynamics playing a critical role in\\nthe proper integration of neurons into functional networks 5     . These network dynamics\\nsequentially unfold while relying on different mechanisms and circuits for their generation\\n1     ,5     . The emergence of functional brain circuits during development is therefore a precisely\\ntimed choreography, the timing of which is inherently tied to the age of the organism under study.\\nHowever, developmental age lacks precision due to significant variations in physical\\ncharacteristics and growth patterns, even among offspring from the same genetic lineage (Fig.\\n1     ). Hence, longitudinal imaging from the same animal across days is the optimal solution to\\nbetter capture the evolution of circuit dynamics during development. Additionally, developmental\\nvariability extends beyond the organism level, becoming even more pronounced at the single\\nneuron level (Fig. 1     ). While population-level descriptions of cortical circuit development are\\ncrucial, individual neurons exhibit unique developmental trajectories rooted in their specific\\norigin, birth timing and cellular identity 1     . In addition, the singular dynamics of sparse\\nindividual neurons can matter, as demonstrated for the rare hub cells 6     ,7     . Thus, in order to\\nfully understand the circuit basis of cortical development in health and disease, it is crucial to\\ntrack neuronal activity at both population and individual cell levels in the growing brain of the\\nsame animal. This hurdle spans both experimental methodology and data analysis.\\nSignificant progress has been made over the recent years to develop innovative solutions to meet\\nthis challenge on the experimental side, using two-photon calcium imaging. Technical\\nimprovements included modified head plates and specialized surgical and care protocols 6     ,8     –\\n12     . Still, in many cases, different neurons from the same mouse were recorded at different ages,\\nor the tracking of individual cells could only be achieved through visual inspection and manual\\nannotation of relatively sparse, often genetically-subscribed neuronal populations expressing a\\ncalcium fluorescent reporter. The gold standard for developmental circuit analysis would be\\nautomated tracking of densely labeled neuronal populations, enabling efficient longitudinal\\nmonitoring while eliminating the burden of manual cell tracking. Performing an acute experiment (dashed grey line)\\nonly provides a single snapshot of the developmental trajectory of an individual organism (or cell). Alternatively, a\\nlongitudinal experiment (grey arrow) allows to track the properties of the same individual (or cell) throughout development,\\nwhich is especially important in the case of variability in developmental trajectories (right).\\n\\n--- PAGE 4 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n4 of 46\\nApplying the algorithm to a dataset recorded during the second postnatal week in mouse barrel\\ncortex, a critical period for the formation of topographic maps in that region, yielded hundreds of\\nidentified neurons tracked across all days. Assessing the quality of the algorithm by benchmarking\\nit on a newly generated ground truth dataset showed high tracking performance 20     . Leveraging\\nground truth benchmarking, we demonstrate that explicitly accounting for developmental\\nprocesses, such as brain growth, are critical for accurately tracking cells during postnatal\\ndevelopment. Our work thus shows that chronic calcium imaging and cell tracking using Track2p\\ncan be used to monitor the changing physiological properties of large populations of matched\\nneurons during early cortical development. We demonstrate that the statistics of activity patterns\\nin the tracked population display two periods of stability, with a critical transition point around\\npostnatal day 11 (P11), marking the emergence of a stable behavioral state representation.\\nResults\\nCell tracking using image registration and overlap-based matching\\nTracking neuronal activity across multiple days presents unique challenges due to the dynamic\\nnature of brain development. We developed a novel tracking algorithm, called Track2p. As in\\nother tracking algorithms for calcium imaging data 13     ,16     ,21     , the final goal of Track2p is to\\nfollow individual cells across sessions allowing the user to compare their functional properties in\\ndownstream analyses. To achieve this goal, the algorithm takes as input a set of preprocessed\\nrecordings, each consisting of a set of regions of interest (ROIs i.e. putative neurons detected based\\non activity, see Methods) and their respective calcium fluorescence traces, as well as a mean image\\nof the field of view (FOV). Briefly, the algorithm aims to match ROIs in any given pair of\\nconsecutive sessions based on their spatial overlap, assuming that the more the two overlap in\\nanatomical space, the more likely they correspond to the same neuron. Due to developmental\\nprocesses such as brain growth and other experimental factors, it is necessary to account for day-\\nto-day changes that occur between the two recordings before computing spatial overlaps. This is\\nachieved by performing affine image registration on the mean FOV images between consecutive\\ndays.\\nWe apply the registration and spatial matching iteratively, starting with the first pair of sessions\\n(s0 and s1) as follows (Fig. 2A     ):\\nFirstly, we estimate the spatial transformation between s0 and s1 using affine image registration\\n(Ntatsis 2023) (i.e. allowing shifting, rotation, scaling and shearing, see Fig. 2B     , the\\ntransformation is denoted as T). We employ affine transformation, since it can account both for\\nrigid transformations (rotations and translations arising from minor mismatches in FOV\\nalignment across experi\\n--- PAGE 5 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n5 of 46\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n5 of 46\\nFigure 2\\nThe Track2p pipeline for tracking cells across recordings.\\nA: Schematic overview of the Track2p algorithm and the GUI capabilities. Dashed squares represent optional steps in the\\npipeline. B: The transformation (T) between two consecutive days is computed by registering the mean FOV images. Top: An\\noverlay of the reference (red, s0) and the moving (green, s1) images before registration. Bottom: Same two images after\\nregistration. Scale bar: 50 μm. C: Applying the transform to ROIs from Suite2p segmentation. The ROI color code is the same\\nas in B, the intersection of the ROIs is shown in yellow. Top: Overlap before T. Bottom: Overlap after T. Note: Only a few\\nexample ROIs are displayed for explanation purposes. D: Cell matching using linear sum assignment. ROIs are the same as C\\nwith indexes for the two recordings added for illustrative purposes. Cells from one recording are matched to cells from\\nanother maximising the sum of the intersection over union (IoU) across all matches. E: Filtering putative false and true\\nmatches by thresholding the IoU distribution. Bottom: Final result of the cell linking for a pair of\\nrecordings. This\\nsuggested that global fluctuations in neural activity are more strongly modulated by active\\nmovement at later than earlier developmental stages.\\nFinally, we asked whether behavioral state could be decoded from population activity dynamics\\nusing regression analysis. We suggest this is mostly due to affine transform being a\\ngood approximation of the growth processes that occur between two consecutive days of imaging,\\nwith the cell matching step allowing to account for slight non-linearities in tissue growth day-by-\\nday. Currently available methods typically register all imaging FOVs to a single reference, which\\nwe believe result in the accumulation of non-linearities making registration more difficult and\\nresulting in poorer tracking performance.\\nFrom a practical perspective, Track2p reduces manual cell tracking time by transforming a tedious\\nprocess of manual annotation into a fast and automated procedure. Indeed, considering the time\\nneeded for manually tracking all cells in our dataset, we estimate that it would require\\napproximately 5 person-days (120 hours) of work per subject under our experimental conditions,\\nhighlighting the necessity of automatic tracking in large scale calcium imaging recordings. To aid\\nfuture research, we provide Track2p fully open-source, with substantial documentation and an\\naccompanying GUI facilitating the use of the algorithm by users without previous coding\\nknowledge.\\nBesides developing the algorithm, we also showcase analysis techniques that can be used to gain\\nunique insights from longitudinal calcium recordings, drawing inspiration from previous research\\nstudying plasticity, learning and representational drift in the adult brain 15     ,35     ,36     . We\\nspecifically highlight: 1) quantifications of functional statistics of the tracked population across\\ndays (Fig 4     ); 2) correlation analyses to compare these across days (Fig 5     ); 3) regression\\nanalyses to study the emergence and stability of neural representations across recording days (Fig\\n6     ).\\nLast, we would like to emphasize that this analytical advancement is grounded on optimized\\nexperimental procedures to image daily cortical dynamics through a glass window mounted on\\nthe developing pup head. Previous studies had described adaptations of the surgical procedure or\\nhead fixation to developing pups 8     , however, by measuring the increase in pairwise distance\\nbetween tracked neurons as a function of age per animal, we now provide a quantitative metric to\\nestimate the invasiveness of the procedure. Interestingly, we observed a growth rate that closely\\nmatched ex vivo quantifications 18     , indicating a minimal impact of our experimental\\npreparation on anatomical brain growth, also confirmed by mouse weight measurements. While\\nwe observed a slightly slower weight gain in imaged pups compared to their littermates, further\\ninvestigation is needed to definitively assess whether repetitive daily imaging might influence\\n--- PAGE 17 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n17 of 46\\n26     ,27     ,29     ,37     ,38     ,49     –52     . Such drastic and fast changes are accompanied by significant\\nbehavioral changes, maybe the most salient ones being the change in the nature and duration of\\nsleep as well as the start of active exploration 53     . The start of behavioural state-modulation and\\ndimensionality increase could reflect the same phenomenon by which spontaneous motor\\nbehavior, including facial movements, drives multidimensional brainwide activity in the adult\\nvisual cortex 30     . This representation is present and stable from P11 onwards, as revealed using\\nour decoding approach. The early absence of representation of spontaneous motor behavior is in\\nagreement with the previously reported lack of reafferent brain activity in response to self-\\ngenerated wake movement until P11 54     –56     . Thus, although we did not explicitly distinguish\\nhere between sleep and wake-generated movements, it is likely that the ‘motion energy’ metric\\ncombined with the decoding mostly took into account wake behavior, given the long periods of\\nmotor activity observed. Hence, we cannot exclude that twitching activity occurring during active\\nsleep could contribute to an earlier representation of spontaneous motion, as reported previously\\n57     . More refined behavioral analysis, particularly of sleep-wake transitions and twitches, could\\nprovide additional insights.\\nTherefore, the mid-second postnatal week marks a transition between two stable functional\\nconnectivity structures, indicating that globally, early and late functional connectivities differ.\\nHowever, singular developmental trajectories cannot be excluded. Future studies could examine\\nwhether different cell types transition at different times or different paces, for example depending\\non their time of birth 1     , or whether unique neurons, such as hub cells, could maintain\\nexceptional and stable functional connectivity 6     . One population of particular interest for\\ntracking are the neurons that contribute the most to behavioral prediction. Indeed, these could\\nform a distinct population of movement-correlated neurons embedded in specific wiring schemes,\\nas recently demonstrated 58     .\\nThis developmental transition centered around P11, which corresponds roughly to birth in human\\nbrain development, spans a period between one and four days, depending on the initial weight of\\nthe imaged pup. This transition may not yet be the last step before a mature adult-like network but\\ninstead represent a transient state 59      preceding the emergence of mature activity patterns. Its\\ntiming, correlated with animal weight, suggests it may represent a conserved developmental\\nmilestone. The second postnatal week marks the emergence of\\nactive sensation, suggesting that changes in arousal state modulation might drive this network\\nreorganization. In the adult cortex, arousal strongly shapes both global firing rates and neural\\ncorrelations 30     –33     , making it a promising candidate for orchestrating the developmental shift\\nwe observed.\\nTo this aim, we sorted the neurons based on the similarity of their activity patterns, using\\nRastermap 34     , and examined the relationship between neuronal activity and behavioral states.\\nWe assessed behavioural state indirectly using the videos capturing spontaneous mouse\\nmovement and quantifying them using a ‘motion energy’ metric (as in 30     , for more details see\\nMethods). This\\nsuggested that global fluctuations in neural activity are more strongly modulated by active\\nmovement at later than earlier developmental stages.\\nFinally, we asked whether behavioral state could be decoded from population activity dynamics\\nusing regression analysis. We suggest this is mostly due to affine transform being a\\ngood approximation of the growth processes that occur between two consecutive days of imaging,\\nwith the cell matching step allowing to account for slight non-linearities in tissue growth day-by-\\nday. Currently available methods typically register all imaging FOVs to a single reference, which\\nwe believe result in the accumulation of non-linearities making registration more difficult and\\nresulting in poorer tracking performance.\\nFrom a practical perspective, Track2p reduces manual cell tracking time by transforming a tedious\\nprocess of manual annotation into a fast and automated procedure. Indeed, considering the time\\nneeded for manually tracking all cells in our dataset, we estimate that it would require\\napproximately 5 person-days (120 hours) of work per subject under our experimental conditions,\\nhighlighting the necessity of automatic tracking in large scale calcium imaging recordings. To aid\\nfuture research, we provide Track2p fully open-source, with substantial documentation and an\\naccompanying GUI facilitating the use of the algorithm by users without previous coding\\nknowledge.\\nBesides developing the algorithm, we also showcase analysis techniques that can be used to gain\\nunique insights from longitudinal calcium recordings, drawing inspiration from previous research\\nstudying plasticity, learning and representational drift in the adult brain 15     ,35     ,36     . We\\nspecifically highlight: 1) quantifications of functional statistics of the tracked population across\\ndays (Fig 4     ); 2) correlation analyses to compare these across days (Fig 5     ); 3) regression\\nanalyses to study the emergence and stability of neural representations across recording days (Fig\\n6     ).\\nLast, we would like to emphasize that this analytical advancement is grounded on optimized\\nexperimental procedures to image daily cortical dynamics through a glass window mounted on\\nthe developing pup head. Previous studies had described adaptations of the surgical procedure or\\nhead fixation to developing pups 8     , however, by measuring the increase in pairwise distance\\nbetween tracked neurons as a function of age per animal, we now provide a quantitative metric to\\nestimate the invasiveness of the procedure. Interestingly, we observed a growth rate that closely\\nmatched ex vivo quantifications 18     , indicating a minimal impact of our experimental\\npreparation on anatomical brain growth, also confirmed by mouse weight measurements. While\\nwe observed a slightly slower weight gain in imaged pups compared to their littermates, further\\ninvestigation is needed to definitively assess whether repetitive daily imaging might influence\\n--- PAGE 17 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n17 of 46\\n26     ,27     ,29     ,37     ,38     ,49     –52     . Such drastic and fast changes are accompanied by significant\\nbehavioral changes, maybe the most salient ones being the change in the nature and duration of\\nsleep as well as the start of active exploration 53     . The start of behavioural state-modulation and\\ndimensionality increase could reflect the same phenomenon by which spontaneous motor\\nbehavior, including facial movements, drives multidimensional brainwide activity in the adult\\nvisual cortex 30     . This representation is present and stable from P11 onwards, as revealed using\\nour decoding approach. The early absence of representation of spontaneous motor behavior is in\\nagreement with the previously reported lack of reafferent brain activity in response to self-\\ngenerated wake movement until P11 54     –56     . Thus, although we did not explicitly distinguish\\nhere between sleep and wake-generated movements, it is likely that the ‘motion energy’ metric\\ncombined with the decoding mostly took into account wake behavior, given the long periods of\\nmotor activity observed. Hence, we cannot exclude that twitching activity occurring during active\\nsleep could contribute to an earlier representation of spontaneous motion, as reported previously\\n57     . More refined behavioral analysis, particularly of sleep-wake transitions and twitches, could\\nprovide additional insights.\\nTherefore, the mid-second postnatal week marks a transition between two stable functional\\nconnectivity structures, indicating that globally, early and late functional connectivities differ.\\nHowever, singular developmental trajectories cannot be excluded. Future studies could examine\\nwhether different cell types transition at different times or different paces, for example depending\\non their time of birth 1     , or whether unique neurons, such as hub cells, could maintain\\nexceptional and stable functional connectivity 6     . One population of particular interest for\\ntracking are the neurons that contribute the most to behavioral prediction. Indeed, these could\\nform a distinct population of movement-correlated neurons embedded in specific wiring schemes,\\nas recently demonstrated 58     .\\nThis developmental transition centered around P11, which corresponds roughly to birth in human\\nbrain development, spans a period between one and four days, depending on the initial weight of\\nthe imaged pup. This transition may not yet be the last step before a mature adult-like network but\\ninstead represent a transient state 59      preceding the emergence of mature activity patterns. Its\\ntiming, correlated with animal weight, suggests it may represent a conserved developmental\\nmilestone. For the\\npurposes of evaluation we used a fully automatic tracking procedure (without manual curation\\nand with default Track2p parameters).\\nDifferent metrics exist for evaluating cell tracking. Since we are aiming to track cells across all\\ndays, a robust cell tracking metric should reward perfect track matches with ground truth while\\nimposing penalties for missed or incorrectly identified tracks. For this reason we used a\\nbiologically inspired ‘complete tracks’ quality metric, which corresponds to the F1 score for\\ncompletely reconstructed full tracks (we refer to this value as ‘CT’ according to 20     ,25     ). A CT\\nscore of 1 would correspond to perfect tracking, while a CT score of 0 would correspond to no\\nmatches or a large proportion of wrong matches (see Fig. 4A      and Methods for more details).\\nAdditionally, to assess tracking performance over time, we quantified the proportion of\\nreconstructed ground truth tracks over progressively longer time intervals (first two days, first\\nthree days etc. ‘Prop. correct’ in Fig. 4C-F     , see Methods). This allowed u\\n--- PAGE 9 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n9 of 46\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n9 of 46\\nFigure 4\\nEvaluation of tracking performance on a manually tracked ground truth dataset\\nduring the second postnatal week of development in the mouse neocortex.\\nA: A schematic representation of possible cases when comparing ground truth tracks (GT, solid lines) with those\\nreconstructed by Track2p (T2P, dashed lines). The CT metric favours perfect matches (top row) and penalises all types of\\nmismatches (bottom four rows). B: Graph showing the CT score for all three GT datasets across evaluated conditions. Mean\\nCT scores of 0.93, 0.91, 0.22 and 0.00 for ‘Anatomical’, ‘Functional’, ‘Rigid’ and ‘CellReg’ respectively. Blue denotes the\\nexample mouse illustrated in Fig. 3     . Interestingly however, these\\nmethods still managed to correctly reconstruct a portion of tracks across shorter age spans in\\ncertain cases (Fig. 4E and F     ), with the performance dropping significantly for longer tracks in\\ncomparison to the baseline condition (Fig. 4C and D     ).\\nThese comparisons demonstrate Track2p’s robust cell tracking performance in the developing\\nbrain, maintaining high accuracy over extended periods of development. However, tracking\\nperformance certainly depends on the type of experimental data (age, model system, brain area,\\nimaging parameters, FOV alignment etc.), hence we also provide additional resources and\\ndocumentation (https://track2p.github.io/home.html     ), allowing users to benchmark Track2p\\ntracking for their particular use case.\\nDevelopment of firing statistics from hundreds of\\ntracked neurons across postnatal development\\nHaving validated the performance of cell tracking, we next analyzed the development of\\nfunctional properties of the tracked population of neurons across the second postnatal week of\\nmouse barrel cortex development. For this, we used a full dataset of 6 mice imaged daily for a\\nminimum of 6 consecutive days within the second postnatal week (P7 to P14, see Fig. 5B      for\\nsummary, we used this dataset for all subsequent analyses). On average 526 (± 190 std) neurons\\nper mouse were successfully tracked across all days using Track2p, corresponding to 33 % (± 11 %\\nstd) of the neurons detected on the first day for each mouse. Based on the high tracking\\nperformance on ground truth (see Fig 4     ) we conclude that this drop is due to a failure in\\ndetection rather than tracking. During the course of the experiment, the weight of a mouse\\nincreased on average by 55% (± 18% std), with the pairwise distance between neurons increasing\\non average by 15% (± 7% std) (Fig. 5C      and Fig. S5F     ). Weight was found to vary significantly\\nacross mice, as early as the first experimental day, and to correlate with brain growth (r=0.92, Fig.\\nS5E     ). Our robust tracking capability provides a comprehensive view of the evolution of\\nneuronal dynamics within single mice, while also allowing us to take into account the\\nheterogeneity of the developmental timelines across mice.\\nThe early postnatal period of cortical development studied here is characterized by the transition\\nfrom synchronous calcium events recruiting many neurons to progressively more decorrelated\\npopulation dynamics 26     ,27     . Interestingly, we observed an increase in the number of\\ncomponents required to explain a fixed amount of variance in the neural data across days,\\nsuggesting a developmental increase in dimensionality (Fig. 5G     ), consistent with the statistics of\\nspontaneous activity described in the adult brain 29     . Notably, the plots of summary statistics for\\nour entire dataset, indicated a clear outlier, consistent across all quantifications (Fig. 5D, E, F and\\nG     ). This outlier mouse displayed a similar but delayed developmental trajectory, compared to\\nthe other mice. Interestingly, it also showed the lowest initial weight and a less pronounced\\ngrowth in weight and cortical size (Fig. 5C     ), likely indicating a lower maturation stage at the\\nonset of the experiments, although the contribution of other experimental factors cannot be\\nexcluded given the invasiveness of imaging surgery.\\nTo fully leverage our longitudinal approach, we next turned to comparing the functional\\nproperties of the same neurons across development, which is only possible when tracking cells\\nacross days.\\nA marked reorganisation of the functional network\\nstructure during the second postnatal week\\nWe first examined the stability of the correlation structure, which we will refer to as ‘Functional\\nconnectivity’ (FC). Two alternative possibilities could be envisaged (see Fig. 6A     ,: (i) FC could be\\nconserved across days, meaning that a highly connected pair on a given day would remain highly\\nconnected on the next (Fig. 6A     , left); (ii) FC could reorganise, losing the structure from the\\nprevious day (Fig. 6A     , right). In the first case, the FC values for all pairs would remain similar\\nacross the two days (Fig. 6A     , bottom left) whereas in the second case they would be different\\n(Fig. 6A     , bottom right). To discriminate between the two cases, we defined a ‘FC similarity’ score\\nfor any pair of imaging days with matched neurons for a given mouse (Pearson correlation (r)\\nacross all neuron pairs, see Fig. 6A     , bottom). Such FC similarity metric, encapsulates a variety of\\npossible sources of network changes, from local or long-range connectivity, intrinsic excitability to\\nneuromodulation.\\nQuantifying FC similarity across two early sessions for an example animal (P8 to P9, Fig. 6B      top\\nleft) indicated remarkable stability, almost identical to the FC similarity within a given imaging\\nday session which we took as a reference (computed as FC similarity between the first and second\\nhalf of a same day recording, Fig 6B     , bottom left). Conversely when comparing across more\\ndistant developmental ages, we noticed that a large part of the correlation structure was lost,\\nresulting in lower FC similarity (P8 to P14, Fig. 6B     , top middle). Additionally, this\\nshows how tracking cells during development opens new analysis possibilities using self-\\nreferencing between neurons, providing new insights into how the developmental choreography\\nunfolds in the same population of neurons across days.\\nDevelopmental emergence of stable behavioral state modulation\\nAs a first attempt to investigate the mechanisms underlying this network transition, we examined\\nneural activity in relation to behavioral state. The second postnatal week marks the emergence of\\nactive sensation, suggesting that changes in arousal state modulation might drive this network\\nreorganization. In the adult cortex, arousal strongly shapes both global firing rates and neural\\ncorrelations 30     –33     , making it a promising candidate for orchestrating the developmental shift\\nwe observed.\\nTo this aim, we sorted the neurons based on the similarity of their activity patterns, using\\nRastermap 34     , and examined the relationship between neuronal activity and behavioral states.\\nWe assessed behavioural state indirectly using the videos capturing spontaneous mouse\\nmovement and quantifying them using a ‘motion energy’ metric (as in 30     , for more details see\\nMethods). Interestingly, this sorting revealed a subpopulation of neurons that were highly\\ncorrelated with the animal’s motion at later, but not earlier developmental stages (Fig. 5A     , see\\nFig. S4A      for all days). We first quantified this relationship by computing the temporal\\ncorrelation between the first principal component of neuronal activity with the animal’s motion.\\nInterestingly, this correlation showed a steep increase after P11 for most mice (Fig. 7D     ). This\\nsuggested that global fluctuations in neural activity are more strongly modulated by active\\nmovement at later than earlier developmental stages.\\nFinally, we asked whether behavioral state could be decoded from population activity dynamics\\nusing regression analysis. Despite substantial variability, the same-day decoding performance\\nincreased with development (Fig. 7A-C     ). Since we tracked the same neurons across days, we\\ncould also probe the stability of this representation across days, by fitting a model on one day and\\ntesting it on all other days (cross-day decoding, Fig. 7E-H     , Fig. S7B      and C     15     ,35     ).\\nInterestingly, this analysis showed that, once developed, this representation was indeed stable,\\nallowing for accurate cross-day decoding, with the same neurons showing either consistently\\npositive or negative modulation across days (see Fig. S7D      for the activity traces of an example\\ncell with a high weight for decoding).\\nHence, by combining Track2p longitudinal cell tracking with decoding approaches, one can\\nsystematica\\n--- PAGE 15 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n15 of 46\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n15 of 46\\nFigure 7\\nRegression analysis to study the development and stability of neural representations.\\nA: Left: A decoding model is fitted on each day to predict a behavioural variable (y, mouse motion) from the simultaneously\\nrecorded calcium imaging data (X, activity raster). We suggest this is mostly due to affine transform being a\\ngood approximation of the growth processes that occur between two consecutive days of imaging,\\nwith the cell matching step allowing to account for slight non-linearities in tissue growth day-by-\\nday. Currently available methods typically register all imaging FOVs to a single reference, which\\nwe believe result in the accumulation of non-linearities making registration more difficult and\\nresulting in poorer tracking performance.\\nFrom a practical perspective, Track2p reduces manual cell tracking time by transforming a tedious\\nprocess of manual annotation into a fast and automated procedure. Indeed, considering the time\\nneeded for manually tracking all cells in our dataset, we estimate that it would require\\napproximately 5 person-days (120 hours) of work per subject under our experimental conditions,\\nhighlighting the necessity of automatic tracking in large scale calcium imaging recordings. To aid\\nfuture research, we provide Track2p fully open-source, with substantial documentation and an\\naccompanying GUI facilitating the use of the algorithm by users without previous coding\\nknowledge.\\nBesides developing the algorithm, we also showcase analysis techniques that can be used to gain\\nunique insights from longitudinal calcium recordings, drawing inspiration from previous research\\nstudying plasticity, learning and representational drift in the adult brain 15     ,35     ,36     . We\\nspecifically highlight: 1) quantifications of functional statistics of the tracked population across\\ndays (Fig 4     ); 2) correlation analyses to compare these across days (Fig 5     ); 3) regression\\nanalyses to study the emergence and stability of neural representations across recording days (Fig\\n6     ).\\nLast, we would like to emphasize that this analytical advancement is grounded on optimized\\nexperimental procedures to image daily cortical dynamics through a glass window mounted on\\nthe developing pup head. Previous studies had described adaptations of the surgical procedure or\\nhead fixation to developing pups 8     , however, by measuring the increase in pairwise distance\\nbetween tracked neurons as a function of age per animal, we now provide a quantitative metric to\\nestimate the invasiveness of the procedure. Interestingly, we observed a growth rate that closely\\nmatched ex vivo quantifications 18     , indicating a minimal impact of our experimental\\npreparation on anatomical brain growth, also confirmed by mouse weight measurements. While\\nwe observed a slightly slower weight gain in imaged pups compared to their littermates, further\\ninvestigation is needed to definitively assess whether repetitive daily imaging might influence\\n--- PAGE 17 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n17 of 46\\n26     ,27     ,29     ,37     ,38     ,49     –52     . Such drastic and fast changes are accompanied by significant\\nbehavioral changes, maybe the most salient ones being the change in the nature and duration of\\nsleep as well as the start of active exploration 53     . The start of behavioural state-modulation and\\ndimensionality increase could reflect the same phenomenon by which spontaneous motor\\nbehavior, including facial movements, drives multidimensional brainwide activity in the adult\\nvisual cortex 30     . This representation is present and stable from P11 onwards, as revealed using\\nour decoding approach. The early absence of representation of spontaneous motor behavior is in\\nagreement with the previously reported lack of reafferent brain activity in response to self-\\ngenerated wake movement until P11 54     –56     . Thus, although we did not explicitly distinguish\\nhere between sleep and wake-generated movements, it is likely that the ‘motion energy’ metric\\ncombined with the decoding mostly took into account wake behavior, given the long periods of\\nmotor activity observed. Hence, we cannot exclude that twitching activity occurring during active\\nsleep could contribute to an earlier representation of spontaneous motion, as reported previously\\n57     . More refined behavioral analysis, particularly of sleep-wake transitions and twitches, could\\nprovide additional insights.\\nTherefore, the mid-second postnatal week marks a transition between two stable functional\\nconnectivity structures, indicating that globally, early and late functional connectivities differ.\\nHowever, singular developmental trajectories cannot be excluded. Future studies could examine\\nwhether different cell types transition at different times or different paces, for example depending\\non their time of birth 1     , or whether unique neurons, such as hub cells, could maintain\\nexceptional and stable functional connectivity 6     . One population of particular interest for\\ntracking are the neurons that contribute the most to behavioral prediction. Indeed, these could\\nform a distinct population of movement-correlated neurons embedded in specific wiring schemes,\\nas recently demonstrated 58     .\\nThis developmental transition centered around P11, which corresponds roughly to birth in human\\nbrain development, spans a period between one and four days, depending on the initial weight of\\nthe imaged pup. This transition may not yet be the last step before a mature adult-like network but\\ninstead represent a transient state 59      preceding the emergence of mature activity patterns. Its\\ntiming, correlated with animal weight, suggests it may represent a conserved developmental\\nmilestone. Indeed, these could\\nform a distinct population of movement-correlated neurons embedded in specific wiring schemes,\\nas recently demonstrated 58     .\\nThis developmental transition centered around P11, which corresponds roughly to birth in human\\nbrain development, spans a period between one and four days, depending on the initial weight of\\nthe imaged pup. This transition may not yet be the last step before a mature adult-like network but\\ninstead represent a transient state 59      preceding the emergence of mature activity patterns. Its\\ntiming, correlated with animal weight, suggests it may represent a conserved developmental\\nmilestone. Hence, comparative studies across brain regions and species, including potential\\nparallels in human development, would be valuable.\\nWe believe that combining Track2p tracking and the analysis methods described here provides a\\ntemplate for future investigations of more complex developmental phenomena, such as the\\nemergence of sensory representations and cognitive functions. Also, longitudinal imaging\\nuniquely enables investigation of activity-dependent development, including how early activity\\npatterns predict later functional properties and assembly formation of the same cells. Indeed,\\nthere is nothing precluding further studies to continue tracking the activity of the same cells until\\nadulthood. Additionally, this approach opens possibilities for targeted manipulation studies to\\nexamine how early perturbations affect subsequent circuit development. The ability to track the\\nsame cells throughout early postnatal development should thus open doors to entirely new classes\\nof experiments not possible before, providing deeper mechanistic insights into developmental\\nprinciples and pathologies. This is even more important considering that alterations of\\ndevelopmental trajectories at early postnatal time points are \\n\\n--- PAGE 19 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n19 of 46\\nrecording days. During the course of each recording, pups were kept warm by a heating element\\nmounted to the imaging setup. After each imaging session, the pups were returned to their home\\ncage with the dam and their littermates.\\nVideography\\nAll videography was performed using a Basler camera (Basler ACE2 1920), with an infrared LED\\nlight source (ThorLabs 850 nm) pointed at the mouse. Videos were recorded at 30 Hz, with the\\nmicroscope acquisition acting as a trigger for camera frame acquisition, also allowing for simple\\nsynchronisation across the two modalities. All acquisition was done using custom python scripts\\nusing the PyPylon wrapper for the Basler camera software (Pylon Camera Software Suite, https://\\ngithub.com/basler/pypylon     ).\\nProcessing of calcium imaging data\\nCalcium imaging data was preprocessed using the Suite2p pipeline, sequentially performing\\nmotion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction and spike deconvolution 23      for each\\nrecording separately. Suite2p additionally provides a cell classification feature, providing a\\nprobability of a classifier categorising an ROI as being a true cell. We considered all ROIs above the\\ndefault threshold of 0.5 as true cells. We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F\\n(using the default Suite2p parameters) for all subsequent analyses. To facilitate the use of Track2p\\nwith other preprocessing pipelines, we implemented a data loader that takes as input three simple\\nNumPy arrays corresponding to ROIs, field of view and traces (for more information see: https://\\ntrack2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters     ).\\nPreprocessing videography\\nWe used the global movements of the mouse as a proxy of its arousal state. Similarly as in 62      we\\nquantified these by looking at the pixel-wise difference of consecutive frames in the videography\\nrecording. Namely, we first took each two consecutive frames, computed their pixelwise\\ndifference. We then squared all individual pixel-wise values and summed across pixels. This\\nyielded a scalar value quantifying the motion of the mouse at each time point, which was used for\\nall subsequent analyses.\\nCell tracking\\nImage and ROI registration\\nAs explained in the main text, Track2p aligns the ROIs based on mean FOV image registration. The\\nimplementation allows the user to choose which channel to use for computing the transformation\\n(‘anatomical’ or ‘functional’ channel), as well as which types of transformation to estimate (rigid\\nor affine). Unless otherwise stated, we used the ‘anatomical’ (tdTomato in GAD67-Cre expressing\\ncells) channel with affine transformation (referred to as the ‘baseline’ condition). For the purposes\\nof evaluation we additionally ran tracking using the functional channel with affine registration\\n(‘functional’ in Fig.4     ) and using the anatomical channel with rigid registration (‘rigid’ in\\nFig.4     ). Once registering the images, we applied the same transform to all ROIs from the\\nsubsequent session that were considered as cells (see main text). All Track2p image registration is\\ndone using the itk-elastix toolbox 63     .\\n\\n\\n--- PAGE 22 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n22 of 46\\ncentroids for each corresponding pair of neurons. When plotting the pairwise correlations as a\\nfunction of pairwise distance we averaged in bins of 30 µm. The correlation of neighbouring\\nneurons was estimated as the intercept of an exponential fit to the full data.\\nWhen comparing the stability of the correlation structure across days, we refer to this as ‘FC\\nstability’, which we calculate as the Pearson correlation between all pairs of original correlation\\nmatrices for a given mouse. When fitting a sigmoid using the first day as the reference, we fix the\\nupper limit of the sigmoid to within-day FC stability for that day and leave the other parameters\\nfree. We calculate the inflection point (‘transition age’) and the linear portion (‘transition time’) of\\nthe sigmoid using the extrema of the first and fourth derivatives respectively.\\nFor principal component analysis (PCA) we used the scikit-learn implementation (Pedregosa 2012,\\nPython).\\nDecoding\\nAll decoding was done using linear regression with ridge regularisation (ridge regression) to avoid\\noverfitting given the large number of neurons. Ridge regression optimises the weights (β) that\\nminimise the following loss function:\\nWhere in our case y is behavioural data, X is neural data and λ is a regularisation parameter. To\\nchoose the optimal λ and to reliably estimate the model performance on same-day decoding we\\nused nested cross-validation (see for example 65     ,66     ), ensuring data efficiency and a splitting\\nbetween training, validation and test data. Briefly, a grid search for the optimal λ is performed by\\nusing the train set to fit models with different λ values and choosing the value corresponding to\\nthe model with the best performance on the held out validation set (model selection). This model is\\nthen evaluated on the test data that was used neither in model fitting nor in the hyperparameter\\noptimization (model evaluation). To ensure data efficiency, nested cross-validation repeats this\\nprocedure for all possible splits of the data, with an outer cross-validation loop used for model\\nevaluation and an inner loop for model selection. We used 5 fold splits for both the inner and\\nouter loops, splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks of the recording. For cross-day\\ndecoding we fit a new model with the optimal lambda for that given day and evaluated it on all\\nother days. For all decoding analysis we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces\\nby averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps. All models were implemented and fitted using\\nPyTorch (Python).\\nData availability\\nAll code is available at the Track2p repository: https://github.com/juremaj/track2p     , with more\\nextensive documentation and demos available at: https://track2p.github.io/home.html     . The data\\nincluding pre-processed neural data for the tracked cells, behavioural data and ground truth data\\nfor cell tracking are available at: https://zenodo.org/records/17091226     .\\nSupplementary information\\nSupplementary Figures\\n\\n\\n--- PAGE 29 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n29 of 46\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n29 of 46\\nSupplementary Figure 7.\\nRelationship between spontaneous behaviour and neural activity for all mice in the dataset.\\nA. Cross-validated same day prediction for all recording days in the example mouse (related to Fig. 6B      and C     ). B:\\nPrediction of a decoder fit on P14 across all days (related to Fig. 6F      and G     ). C. Prediction of a decoder fit on P8 across all\\ndays (related to Fig. 6F      and G     ). D. Example dF/F trace of a highly predictive neuron for a model fit at P14. Notice that the\\nactivity closely corresponds to the movement trace also on two earlier days (P12 and P13). E. Full matrices of R2 values for all\\ncombinations of train and test recordings in each mouse. Example mouse (*). F. Mean R2 matrix averaged across all mice in\\nthe dataset. G. Box plots of the distribution of individual R2 values in different conditions pooled across mice (same as Fig.\\n5E     ). H. Statistical significance of pairwise comparisons of data from G. I: P values for all statistical comparisons of decoder\\nperformance for all comparisons (related D and E in this figure and Fig 7     . H).\\n\\n[stdout]\\nwarning: The `fitz` API is deprecated and will be removed in future. Use `import pymupdf` instead.\\n--- PAGE 15 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n15 of 46\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n15 of 46\\nFigure 7\\nRegression analysis to study the development and stability of neural representations.\\nA: Left: A decoding model is fitted on each day to predict a behavioural variable (y, mouse motion) from the simultaneously\\nrecorded calcium imaging data (X, activity raster). Right: The model is then tested on the same day (using cross-validation) to\\nassess the encoding of the variable on that given day. B: Overlay of animal’s motion (grey) and the predicted signal from\\nneural activity (green) fit on the same day for example early (P8, top) and late recordings (P14, bottom). Symbols indicate the\\ncorresponding R2 values in panels C and G. Only the first 5 min of the recording are shown for visualisation purposes, for full\\ntraces of all recordings see Fig. S7     . C: Graphs indicating R2 values for same-day cross-validated decoding performance as a\\nfunction of mouse age. Green indicates the example mouse (also in D). D: Graph plotting the correlation between the\\nanimal’s motion and the first principal component signal across days (left) and a statistical comparison of the early (≤ P11)\\nand late (> P11) epochs (right). *: Mann–Whitney U test, p = 1.2 × 10−3. E: Left: Same as in A an individual model is fitted on\\neach day. Right: To assess the stability of the representation, each model is tested across different days, which is only\\npossible when tracking neurons across sessions. F: Same as B but for examples of cross-day decoding early to early (P9 to\\nP8), late to late (P13 to P14) and early to late (P8 to P14). Symbols indicate the corresponding R2 values in panel G. G: Full\\nprediction performance matrix showing R2 values for all combinations of fit and test datasets (rows and columns\\nrespectively) for the example mouse (left) and average across all mice for the same period (right). Diagonal entries\\ncorrespond to same-day decoding, off-diagonal entries to cross-day recording. H: Box plots comparing R2 values for same\\nday (left) or across-day (right) decoding for early (≤ P11) and late (> P11) developmental epochs pooled across all mice.*:\\nstatistically significant; ns: not statistically significant (Kruskall-Wallis test: p = 4.5 × 10−10; post-hoc Mann–Whitney U tests\\nwith Bonferroni correction, early within - late within: p = 1.6 × 10−3, early to early - late to late: p = 6.5 × 10−6, early to early -\\nearly to late: p = n. s., late to late - early to late: p = 2.4 × 10−5, for all possible comparisons see Fig. S7I     ).\\n\\n\\n--- PAGE 17 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n17 of 46\\n26     ,27     ,29     ,37     ,38     ,49     –52     . Such drastic and fast changes are accompanied by significant\\nbehavioral changes, maybe the most salient ones being the change in the nature and duration of\\nsleep as well as the start of active exploration 53     . The start of behavioural state-modulation and\\ndimensionality increase could reflect the same phenomenon by which spontaneous motor\\nbehavior, including facial movements, drives multidimensional brainwide activity in the adult\\nvisual cortex 30     . This representation is present and stable from P11 onwards, as revealed using\\nour decoding approach. The early absence of representation of spontaneous motor behavior is in\\nagreement with the previously reported lack of reafferent brain activity in response to self-\\ngenerated wake movement until P11 54     –56     . Thus, although we did not explicitly distinguish\\nhere between sleep and wake-generated movements, it is likely that the ‘motion energy’ metric\\ncombined with the decoding mostly took into account wake behavior, given the long periods of\\nmotor activity observed. Hence, we cannot exclude that twitching activity occurring during active\\nsleep could contribute to an earlier representation of spontaneous motion, as reported previously\\n57     . More refined behavioral analysis, particularly of sleep-wake transitions and twitches, could\\nprovide additional insights.\\nTherefore, the mid-second postnatal week marks a transition between two stable functional\\nconnectivity structures, indicating that globally, early and late functional connectivities differ.\\nHowever, singular developmental trajectories cannot be excluded. This\\nyielded a scalar value quantifying the motion of the mouse at each time point, which was used for\\nall subsequent analyses.\\nCell tracking\\nImage and ROI registration\\nAs explained in the main text, Track2p aligns the ROIs based on mean FOV image registration. The\\nimplementation allows the user to choose which channel to use for computing the transformation\\n(‘anatomical’ or ‘functional’ channel), as well as which types of transformation to estimate (rigid\\nor affine). Unless otherwise stated, we used the ‘anatomical’ (tdTomato in GAD67-Cre expressing\\ncells) channel with affine transformation (referred to as the ‘baseline’ condition). For the purposes\\nof evaluation we additionally ran tracking using the functional channel with affine registration\\n(‘functional’ in Fig.4     ) and using the anatomical channel with rigid registration (‘rigid’ in\\nFig.4     ). Once registering the images, we applied the same transform to all ROIs from the\\nsubsequent session that were considered as cells (see main text). All Track2p image registration is\\ndone using the itk-elastix toolbox 63     .\\n\\n\\n--- PAGE 22 ---\\nJure Majnik et al., 2025 eLife. https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.2\\n22 of 46\\ncentroids for each corresponding pair of neurons. When plotting the pairwise correlations as a\\nfunction of pairwise distance we averaged in bins of 30 µm. The correlation of neighbouring\\nneurons was estimated as the intercept of an exponential fit to the full data.\\nWhen comparing the stability of the correlation structure across days, we refer to this as ‘FC\\nstability’, which we calculate as the Pearson correlation between all pairs of original correlation\\nmatrices for a given mouse. When fitting a sigmoid using the first day as the reference, we fix the\\nupper limit of the sigmoid to within-day FC stability for that day and leave the other parameters\\nfree. We calculate the inflection point (‘transition age’) and the linear portion (‘transition time’) of\\nthe sigmoid using the extrema of the first and fourth derivatives respectively.\\nFor principal component analysis (PCA) we used the scikit-learn implementation (Pedregosa 2012,\\nPython).\\nDecoding\\nAll decoding was done using linear regression with ridge regularisation (ridge regression) to avoid\\noverfitting given the large number of neurons. Ridge regression optimises the weights (β) that\\nminimise the following loss function:\\nWhere in our case y is behavioural data, X is neural data and λ is a regularisation parameter. To\\nchoose the optimal λ and to reliably estimate the model performance on same-day decoding we\\nused nested cross-validation (see for example 65     ,66     ), ensuring data efficiency and a splitting\\nbetween training, validation and test data. Briefly, a grid search for the optimal λ is performed by\\nusing the train set to fit models with different λ values and choosing the value corresponding to\\nthe model with the best performance on the held out validation set (model selection). This model is\\nthen evaluated on the test data that was used neither in model fitting nor in the hyperparameter\\noptimization (model evaluation). To ensure data efficiency, nested cross-validation repeats this\\nprocedure for all possible splits of the data, with an outer cross-validation loop used for model\\nevaluation and an inner loop for model selection. We used 5 fold splits for both the inner and\\nouter loops, splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks of the recording. For cross-day\\ndecoding we fit a new model with the optimal lambda for that given day and evaluated it on all\\nother days. For all decoding analysis we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces\\nby averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps. All models were implemented and fitted using\\nPyTorch (Python).\\nData availability\\nAll code is available at the Track2p repository: https://github.com/juremaj/track2p     , with more\\nextensive documentation and demos available at: https://track2p.github.io/home.html     . Such drastic and fast changes are accompanied by significant\\nbehavioral changes, maybe the most salient ones being the change in the nature and duration of\\nsleep as well as the start of active exploration 53     . The start of behavioural state-modulation and\\ndimensionality increase could reflect the same phenomenon by which spontaneous motor\\nbehavior, including facial movements, drives multidimensional brainwide activity in the adult\\nvisual cortex 30     . This representation is present and stable from P11 onwards, as revealed using\\nour decoding approach. The early absence of representation of spontaneous motor behavior is in\\nagreement with the previously reported lack of reafferent brain activity in response to self-\\ngenerated wake movement until P11 54     –56     . Thus, although we did not explicitly distinguish\\nhere between sleep and wake-generated movements, it is likely that the ‘motion energy’ metric\\ncombined with the decoding mostly took into account wake behavior, given the long periods of\\nmotor activity observed. Hence, we cannot exclude that twitching activity occurring during active\\nsleep could contribute to an earlier representation of spontaneous motion, as reported previously\\n57     . More refined behavioral analysis, particularly of sleep-wake transitions and twitches, could\\nprovide additional insights.\\nTherefore, the mid-second postnatal week marks a transition between two stable functional\\nconnectivity structures, indicating that globally, early and late functional connectivities differ.\\nHowever, singular developmental trajectories cannot be excluded. Future studies could examine\\nwhether different cell types transition at different times or different paces, for example depending\\non their time of birth 1     , or whether unique neurons, such as hub cells, could maintain\\nexceptional and stable functional connectivity 6     . Indeed, these could\\nform a distinct population of movement-correlated neurons embedded in specific wiring schemes,\\nas recently demonstrated 58     .\\nThis developmental transition centered around P11, which corresponds roughly to birth in human\\nbrain development, spans a period between one and four days, depending on the initial weight of\\nthe imaged pup. This transition may not yet be the last step before a mature adult-like network but\\ninstead represent a transient state 59      preceding the emergence of mature activity patterns. Its\\ntiming, correlated with animal weight, suggests it may represent a conserved developmental\\nmilestone. Hence, comparative studies across brain regions and species, including potential\\nparallels in human development, would be valuable.\\nWe believe that combining Track2p tracking and the analysis methods described here provides a\\ntemplate for future investigations of more complex developmental phenomena, such as the\\nemergence of sensory representations and cognitive functions. Indeed,\\nthere is nothing precluding further studies to continue tracking the activity of the same cells until\\nadulthood.
